## Citation interfaces, group a

The elliptic-form interfaces consistently use a first-slot-linear sesquilinear pairing and conjugate-linear data. The Lax–Milgram uses supply a Hilbert form domain, boundedness and positive coercivity; shifted estimates preserve the full Sobolev norm. Rellich is invoked at p=2 only on bounded zero-trace domains, or bounded connected extension domains for the Neumann mean-zero construction. AC/CC premises are explicit where needed. Coefficient specializations, weak eigenpair identities, adjoint forms and zero-mode exclusions agree with the cited definitions. No extra boundary regularity, eigenbasis simplicity or invertibility is inferred from these citations.

## Citation interfaces, group b

The wave citations use Box_c=partial_t^2-c^2 Delta and the exact speed factors in d’Alembert, Kirchhoff and Poisson. Representation uses meet the dimensional smoothness/CC hypotheses; initial attainment is supplied separately for n>=2. Huygens uses neighbourhood agreement on the sphere and radial jets, and tail witnesses remain strictly inside the base ball. Energy consumers explicitly supply finite energy and vanishing flux rather than infer them from the local identity. The Hardy/BMO interfaces specialize to (1,infinity,0)-atoms, retain distributional and H1 tail convergence, and fix an admissible grand-maximal order; the bounded BMO integral is distinguished from its extension by atom pairings.

## Citation interfaces, group c

Sobolev and Rellich citations preserve n>=2, finite p and the strictly subcritical target exponent; p=1 is closed by smooth zero-extension/density and the GNS endpoint. Higher-order derivatives retain the correct reduced Sobolev order, and compactness uses a fixed bounded support ball. Morrey is applied locally to the global extension, then restricted to the compact closure; the Holder compactness exponent is strictly below 1-n/p. Critical compactness uses the higher-order k=1,p=n supplier for finite targets, rather than an unjustified infinity endpoint.

## Citation interfaces, group d

Viscosity contacts preserve the maximum/subsolution and minimum/supersolution signs, including the relaxed initial trace and coordinate permutation. The Hopf–Lax correspondence uses bounded Lipschitz initial primitives, a finite convex superlinear Hamiltonian, and the normalized primitive minus t f(0); the rarefaction example explicitly excludes its unbounded primitive from that theorem. The compactness consumer establishes common compact support and both space/time translation moduli before invoking Frechet–Kolmogorov at p=1, with DC carrying CC. The parabolic-cylinder citation is used for the initial/lateral boundary and the left derivative at the terminal maximum.

## Citation interfaces, group e

Semigroup citations consistently use A as the generator of e^{tA}, R(lambda,A)=(lambda I-A)^{-1}, and the negative of the positive elliptic form operator for heat flow. Laplace formulas are used beyond a supplied exponential bound; complex-parameter versions are derived locally rather than attributed to the real-parameter supplier. Generator commutation is restricted to D(A), and closedness justifies limits of graph pairs. Bochner linearity/FTC uses have continuous or integrable Banach-valued integrands and oriented intervals. Classical forcing upgrades retain x in D(A) and Holder or Bochner-derivative regularity. Higher smoothing uses graph-valued forcing; spatial Sobolev identifications retain the C^{2m} boundary and iterated trace conditions, with a separate one-dimensional weak-derivative argument. Hilbert dissipativity uses its choice-free quadratic equivalence, while Lumer–Phillips uses density and an actual surjectivity point. AC is stated for the spectral/eigenbasis branch.

## Citation interfaces, group f

Poincare citations use p=2 on zero-trace classes in a finite-width slab, with the full H1 norm factor 1+C_P^2 retained. The Neumann argument uses connected extension-domain Poincare–Wirtinger only on global mean-zero classes; its disconnected clause is separately derived from Rellich and componentwise constants. Character-ring citations use coefficientwise finite products, Weyl action only on finite support, an invertible A(rho), and the sign character (-1)^length. Dominant integral weights and finite-dimensional completely reducible modules meet the Weyl-character hypotheses; minuscule nondominant translates lie on a rho-shifted wall, and Racah–Speiser cancels reflection pairs rather than applying the Weyl action to an infinite cone series.

## Citation interfaces, group g

Elliptic references preserve the componentwise coefficient bounds and complex real-part ellipticity, with weak tests extended by density only when the localized source is integrable in the dual class. Zero-trace/global problems and compactly supported local tests are distinguished. Sobolev uses separate n>=3 critical-exponent bounds from finite-target dimension-two bounds; q>n/2 supplies bounded source functionals before Lax–Milgram barriers. Boundary flattening keeps Jacobian/transposed derivatives and Lipschitz principal coefficients; higher commutators retain one additional coefficient derivative and genuine L2 forcing. Harnack retains nonnegativity, the doubled ball and the additive scale R^{2-n/q}; maximum principles retain c>=0 and the weak sign condition. Counterexamples use explicit weak identities, and the degenerate-matrix citation names only the analogous integral form, explicitly excluding uniform ellipticity. The annulus example meets the current n>=2 De Giorgi–Nash statement, and Moser’s M_a^2 upper bound is part of its standing principal-matrix hypothesis, rather than inferred from componentwise bounds.

## Citation interfaces, group h

Regularity citations keep the distinction between an a priori W2p estimate and weak-to-strong membership. Rellich applies to bounded extension domains with q=p in each exponent regime; higher-order embeddings use the displayed k=2,p extension property and strict Holder ranges. The Laplace Schauder bridge first solves the weak zero-trace problem and then bootstraps through a finite exponent list, without assuming W2p membership. The C1,1 graph citation uses the real-valued Lipschitz converse only on smaller convex base boxes. Character uses evaluate Weyl’s formula at dominant integral highest weights, preserving the dual highest weight -w0 nu and the shifted BWB weight; the SL2 and SL3 dimension computations meet the exact Weyl hypotheses.

## Citation interfaces, group i

Finite Fourier citations preserve counting measure, the negative-sign N^{-1/2} transform, finite Parseval and nonzero norm before division; N=1 is explicitly separated. Variational uses retain real forms/classes and the exact choice premises, Poincare on zero-trace differences, and Rellich before preserving the unit sphere. Smooth minimiser conclusions require the actual lifting/domain hypotheses; the zero-trace classical-to-weak lemma is explicitly not applied to a nonzero-trace classical solution. Obstacle uses retain symmetric/coercive real forms, continuous H^{-1} reaction pairings and an L2 image L psi before applying global truncations. The direct-method convention defines properness on the admissible restriction, providing a finite competitor there; no conclusion is licensed merely by a finite value outside the constraint. Hilbert Riesz is used with first-slot-linear real pairing, and Stampacchia retains nonsymmetric bounded coercivity and handles H={0} separately.

## Citation interfaces, group j

LCA Fourier interfaces preserve the conjugate-phase forward transform and positive-phase inverse transform, fixed Haar normalization and reciprocal dual scale. Positive convolution squares use g in C_c; their dual L1 integrability is invoked only after compatible normalization. Bochner is applied to continuous positive-definite functions with finite positive Radon representing measures. Fourier–Stieltjes uniqueness uses finite regular complex measures and vanishing at every x; density arguments localize nonzero continuous transforms on compact sets, not a countable global dual. Modulation/translation identities are extended from L1 intersect L2 to L2 by density and isometries. The isometric-extension supplier is used without assuming surjectivity; the later range theorem supplies unitarity before the positive dual bump. The bounded-domain heat example retains A=-L, an admissible positive shift, positive eigenvalues, AC/CC, and graph smoothing without a spatial H2 identification.
